Goal 2’: In full contrast with Goal 2 in Section 2.7, the solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{ \kappa _{ q}}$$\end{document}ϑκq does not dissipate energy, and actually it remains essentially unchanged in the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[1-T_{ q }, 1+T_{{ q }}]$$\end{document}[1-Tq,1+Tq].

In order to accomplish Goal 2’ we need two steps. First, the so-called Itô-Tanaka trick (first used in the context of the advection equation with multiplicative noise in [37]) allows to exploit suitable cancellations to show that the stochastic flow remains almost constant on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[1-T_q, 1 - T_{q+1}]$$\end{document}[1-Tq,1-Tq+1]. This relies on the fact that the velocity field on such time interval either vanishes or has frequency at least \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{q+1}$$\end{document}λq+1 and entails the fact that the typical displacement of the Brownian motion is much larger than the inverse of the typical frequency of the velocity field in the time interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[1-T_q, 1- T_{q+1}]$$\end{document}[1-Tq,1-Tq+1], namely2.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sqrt{\kappa _q (T_q - T_{q+1})} \gg \lambda _{q+1}^{-1} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}κq(Tq-Tq+1)≫λq+1-1.Second, we need to require that the solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{\kappa _q}$$\end{document}ϑκq dissipates only a small portion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 norm in the time interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[1-T_q, 1+T_q]$$\end{document}[1-Tq,1+Tq], which entails the constraint\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \kappa _q \lambda _{ q }^2 T_q \ll 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}κqλq2Tq≪1.

Regularity of the Velocity Field and Uniform-in-Diffusivity Bounds on the Passive Scalar

The velocity field rearranges in the time interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[1- T_{q-1}, 1- T_q]$$\end{document}[1-Tq-1,1-Tq] the chessboard of side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{q-1}^{-1}$$\end{document}λq-1-1 into the chessboard of side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _q^{-1}$$\end{document}λq-1, and therefore the associated trajectories have a displacement of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lambda _{q-1}^{-1}$$\end{document}λq-1-1 with frequency concentrated at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _q$$\end{document}λq. This implies by interpolation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} \Vert u(t, \cdot ) \Vert _{C^\alpha ( {{\mathbb {T}}}^2)} \sim {} \Vert u (t, \cdot ) \Vert _{L^\infty ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2)} \lambda _q^\alpha \sim \frac{\lambda _{q-1}^{-1} \lambda _q^\alpha }{ (T_{q-1} - T_q) } \\{} & {} \qquad \text { and } \qquad \Vert \vartheta (t, \cdot ) \Vert _{C^\beta ( {{\mathbb {T}}}^2)} \sim \Vert \vartheta (t, \cdot ) \Vert _{L^\infty ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2)} \lambda _q^\beta \sim \lambda _q^\beta \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u(t,·)‖Cα(T2)∼‖u(t,·)‖L∞(T2)λqα∼λq-1-1λqα(Tq-1-Tq)and‖ϑ(t,·)‖Cβ(T2)∼‖ϑ(t,·)‖L∞(T2)λqβ∼λqβfor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [1-T_{q-1}, 1-T_q]$$\end{document}t∈[1-Tq-1,1-Tq]. As a first consequence, we observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in C^0([0,1] \times {{\mathbb {T}}}^2)$$\end{document}u∈C0([0,1]×T2) as soon as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \sim p^\circ \beta <1$$\end{document}γ∼p∘β<1; the latter condition holds in particular close to the Onsager criticality threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = \alpha ^\circ =1/3$$\end{document}α=α∘=1/3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p= p^{\circ } =3$$\end{document}p=p∘=3. Moreover we estimate2.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \Vert ^p_{L^{p} ([0,1]; C^\beta ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2))$$\end{document}L∞([0,1];Cβ(T2)) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta >0$$\end{document}β>0, which would correspond to the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=1$$\end{document}p=1.

The above heuristics can be made rigorous for solutions of the advection equation (1.1). However, for the corresponding solutions of the advection-diffusion equation (1.2), we are not aware of any general method to prove regularity estimates building on the previous observations. In the proof of Theorem A we develop a new approach to this problem which shows regularity for the stochastic flow uniformly in the stochastic parameter and which strongly relies on the alternating shear-flow structure of the velocity field. Notice that, for a genuinely two-dimensional velocity field as in [4, 62], even though the same scaling as in (2.9) is satisfied, showing uniform-in-diffusivity regularity estimates seems an extremely hard task. In [26] only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\infty $$\end{document}L∞ bounds on the passive scalar are shown to hold uniformly-in-diffusivity as a consequence of direct bounds on the advection-diffusion equation. However, due to results in [5], no fractional-regularity bounds due to functional arguments are expected to hold for the advection-diffusion equation.

We close this section by observing how a suitable choice of the parameters is compatible with the regularity bounds (2.8) and (2.9) in the full supercritical Obukhov-Corrsin regularity range. Assuming a superexponential growth of the frequencies of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _q = \lambda _{q-1}^{1+\delta }$$\end{document}λq=λq-11+δ (where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta >0$$\end{document}δ>0 is a small parameter) and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{q-1} - T_q \sim \lambda _q^{- \gamma }$$\end{document}Tq-1-Tq∼λq-γ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \not \in m{{\mathbb {N}}}$$\end{document}q∉mN (for a suitable paramater \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma >0$$\end{document}γ>0), we observe that the convergence of both sums in (2.8) and (2.9) follows from\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} - p + p \alpha (1+\delta )- \gamma + \gamma p<0, \\ p^{\circ } \beta (1+\delta ) - \gamma <0. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-p+pα(1+δ)-γ+γp<0,p∘β(1+δ)-γ<0.Letting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}δ→0, this system admits a solution for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ provided \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/p + 2/p^\circ =1$$\end{document}1/p+2/p∘=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha + 2 \beta <1$$\end{document}α+2β<1, that is, in the full supercritical Obukhov-Corrsin regularity range. We also observe that the choices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa _q = \lambda _q^{-2}$$\end{document}κq=λq-2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\kappa }_q = \lambda _q^{-2 + \frac{\gamma }{1+ \delta }}$$\end{document}κ~q=λq-2+γ1+δ guarantee the constraints listed in Section 2.6 and Section 2.7 (at least, up to small modifications, for instance in (2.7) we need to require \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \ge \gamma /2$$\end{document}δ≥γ/2).

Notations and Preliminaries

We mostly employ standard general notation. We will work on the 2-dimensional torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathbb {T}}}^n \cong {\mathbb {R}}^n / {{\mathbb {Z}}}^n \cong [0,1]^n / \sim $$\end{document}Tn≅Rn/Zn≅[0,1]n/∼. The only specific notation is the one for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon $$\end{document}ε-restriction of a set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \subset {{\mathbb {T}}}^n $$\end{document}A⊂Tn, defined for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon >0$$\end{document}ε>0 as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A[\varepsilon ] := \{ x \in {{\mathbb {T}}}^n: {{\,\textrm{d}\,}}(x, A^c ) > \varepsilon \}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A[ε]:={x∈Tn:d(x,Ac)>ε}.In all the paper we make systematic use of several notions and results concerning deterministic and stochastic flows, and their relation to advection and advection-diffusion equations. We summarize the main definitions and results in the remaining of this section without any attempt to be complete or systematic. The deterministic theory for smooth velocity fields is fully classical, while for the stochastic theory we refer to [34, 46, 49] and also to [50] for the case of nonsmooth velocity fields.

We also observe for later use that, by means of the classical Grönwall inequality, it is possible to estimate the distance between the deterministic and the stochastic flows associated to the same velocity field as3.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} | {\varvec{X}}_{t} (x) - {\varvec{X}}_t^\kappa (y, \omega )|{} & {} \le \left( |x- y| + \sqrt{2 \kappa } \sup _{s \in [0,t]} | {\varvec{W}}\hspace{-2.22214pt}_s(\omega )| \right) \nonumber \\{} & {} \qquad \exp \left( \int _0^t \Vert \nabla u (s, \cdot ) \Vert _{L^\infty ({{\mathbb {T}}}^n)} ds \right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|Xt(x)-Xtκ(y,ω)|≤|x-y|+2κsups∈[0,t]|Ws(ω)|exp∫0t‖∇u(s,·)‖L∞(Tn)dsfor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}$$\end{document}P-a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω and for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x,y\in {{\mathbb {T}}}^n$$\end{document}x,y∈Tn.

The Feynman-Kac formula provides a representation of the unique bounded solution of the advection-diffusion equation (1.2) via the backward stochastic flow:3.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vartheta _\kappa (t,x) = \mathbb {E}[ \vartheta _{{{\,\textrm{in}\,}}} ({\varvec{X}}^{\kappa }_{0,t}(x ))] \qquad \text {for every }t\ge 0, \text {for a.e.} x\in {{\mathbb {T}}}^n. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϑκ(t,x)=E[ϑin(X0,tκ(x))]for everyt≥0,for a.e.x∈Tn.A formula involving the push-forward via the forward stochastic flow (analogous to (3.2) for the deterministic flow) holds as well: for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \ge 0$$\end{document}t≥03.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{{{\mathbb {T}}}^n} f(x) \vartheta _{\kappa } (t,x) \, dx = \mathbb {E} \int _{{{\mathbb {T}}}^n} f({\varvec{X}}_t^\kappa (x, \cdot )) \vartheta _{{{\,\textrm{in}\,}}} (x) \, dx \qquad \text {for any }f \in L^\infty ({{\mathbb {T}}}^n).\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Tnf(x)ϑκ(t,x)dx=E∫Tnf(Xtκ(x,·))ϑin(x)dxfor anyf∈L∞(Tn).All of the above results are quite classical in the case of smooth velocity fields. A systematic theory in the case of Sobolev velocity fields is presented in [50]. However, the velocity field in our construction will not have such a regularity across the singular time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1. For a velocity field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in L^p ((0,T); L^q({{\mathbb {T}}}^n))$$\end{document}u∈Lp((0,T);Lq(Tn)) under the Ladyžhenskaya–Prodi–Serrin condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2/p + 3/q <1$$\end{document}2/p+3/q<1 it is proven in [48] that a unique stochastic flow exists. To the best of our knowledge, it has not been explicitly shown in the literature that the measure-preserving property (3.5) holds in case the velocity field additionally is divergence-free. We sketch a proof of this fact in a case adapted to our context in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.1

Fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa >0$$\end{document}κ>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (0,1)$$\end{document}α∈(0,1). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in C^\alpha ((0,T) \times {{\mathbb {T}}}^n) $$\end{document}u∈Cα((0,T)×Tn) be a divergence-free velocity field and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{in } \in L^\infty ({{\mathbb {T}}}^n)$$\end{document}ϑin∈L∞(Tn). Then, the unique bounded solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _\kappa $$\end{document}ϑκ to (1.2) is represented by formula (3.11) and the unique stochastic flow satisfies the measure-preserving property (3.5).

Proof

The proof is classical for a smooth divergence-free velocity field and the general result follows from the approximation theorem [37, Theorem 5]. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Construction and Main Properties of the Velocity Field

In this section we introduce all parameters needed in our constructions. Based on the choice of the parameters, we describe the construction of the velocity field in Theorems A, B, and C and the action of the corresponding flow on the solutions. We also collect several useful properties of the velocity field.

Choice of the Parameters

Initial Datum

The initial datum is the same for Theorem A, Theorem B and Theorem C.

Definition 4.1

(Chessboards) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in {{\mathbb {N}}}$$\end{document}λ∈N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _0 : {{\mathbb {T}}}^2 \rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}ϑ0:T2→R be defined as4.16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vartheta _0(x_1, x_2) = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 1 &{} \text { if } x_1, x_2 \in [0,1/2) \text { or } x_1, x_2 \in [1/2,1) \\ -1 &{} \text { otherwise} \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϑ0(x1,x2)=1ifx1,x2∈[0,1/2)orx1,x2∈[1/2,1)-1otherwiseand extended by periodicity. We say that the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta ^{(1)} (x)= \vartheta _0 ( \lambda x )$$\end{document}ϑ(1)(x)=ϑ0(λx) is the even chessboard function of side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2\lambda )^{-1}$$\end{document}(2λ)-1 and the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta ^{(2)} (x)= - \vartheta _0 ( \lambda x )$$\end{document}ϑ(2)(x)=-ϑ0(λx) is the odd chessboard function of side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2\lambda )^{-1}$$\end{document}(2λ)-1. We further say that the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \widetilde{A}_{\lambda } = {{\,\textrm{supp}\,}}\{ 1+ \vartheta ^{(1)} \}$$\end{document}A~λ=supp{1+ϑ(1)} is the even chessboard set of side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2 \lambda )^{-1}$$\end{document}(2λ)-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{B}_{\lambda } = {{\,\textrm{supp}\,}}\{ 1+ \vartheta ^{(2)} \}$$\end{document}B~λ=supp{1+ϑ(2)} is the odd chessboard set of side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2 \lambda )^{-1}$$\end{document}(2λ)-1.

We consider as initial datum a smoothed version of the even chessboard function of side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0$$\end{document}a0: for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _0\in L^\infty ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2)$$\end{document}ϑ0∈L∞(T2) defined as in (4.16) we consider4.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vartheta _{{{\,\textrm{in}\,}}} = \vartheta _0 (\lambda _0 \, \cdot ) *\psi _0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϑin=ϑ0(λ0·)∗ψ0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _0 (x) = \lambda _0^{2 + 2 \epsilon \delta } \psi (\lambda _0^{1 + \epsilon \delta } x)$$\end{document}ψ0(x)=λ02+2ϵδψ(λ01+ϵδx) is the convolution kernel in the spatial variable (used also in Section 4.2).

Main Properties of the Velocity Field

The action of the velocity field has been informally described in Section 2. Up to small (and quantified) errors, on each time interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {I}}_q$$\end{document}Iq (before the critical time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1) the scale of the solution is lowered from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_q$$\end{document}aq to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{q+1}$$\end{document}aq+1, while on each time interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {J}}_q$$\end{document}Jq (after the critical time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document}t=1) the scale of the solution is increased from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{q+1}$$\end{document}aq+1 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_q$$\end{document}aq. The swap velocity field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{{{\,\textrm{swap}\,}}}$$\end{document}uswap (only required in Theorem C) swaps the parity of the chessboard on each time interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {J}}_q$$\end{document}Jq.

In order to quantify the errors due to the regularizations of the velocity field, we define an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_q^{1 + \epsilon \delta }$$\end{document}aq1+ϵδ-restricted version of the even/odd chessboard sets of side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_q$$\end{document}aq and a “good set”. Recalling Definition 4.1, we define4.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A_q = \widetilde{A}_{\lambda _q}[5 a_q^{1+ \epsilon \delta }] \,, \qquad B_q = \widetilde{B}_{\lambda _q}[5 a_q^{1+ \epsilon \delta }] \,, \qquad G_q = A_q \cup B_q \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Aq=A~λq[5aq1+ϵδ],Bq=B~λq[5aq1+ϵδ],Gq=Aq∪Bq.We observe that for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q\in {{\mathbb {N}}}$$\end{document}q∈N there hold4.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}^2 (A_q) = \mathcal {L}^2 (B_q) \ge \frac{1}{2} - 10 a_q^{\epsilon \delta } \qquad \text { and } \qquad \mathcal {L}^2(G_q) \ge 1 - 20 a_q^{\epsilon \delta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L2(Aq)=L2(Bq)≥12-10aqϵδandL2(Gq)≥1-20aqϵδ.The above estimates are proved by elementary geometric considerations. For instance, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}^2 (G^c_{q})$$\end{document}L2(Gqc) is estimated by the measure of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_q^{-1}$$\end{document}aq-1 vertical stripes of measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10 a_q^{1+ \epsilon \delta }$$\end{document}10aq1+ϵδ plus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_q^{-1}$$\end{document}aq-1 horizontal stripes of measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10 a_q^{1+ \epsilon \delta }$$\end{document}10aq1+ϵδ. Remark 4.2

Proof of Theorem C

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in [0,1[$$\end{document}α∈[0,1[ as in the statement of Theorem B, we choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =0$$\end{document}β=0 and recall that all relevant parameters have been correspondingly fixed in Section 4.1. In particular, we recall that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a_0^{\epsilon \delta /8 } \le \frac{1}{20} \,, \quad \epsilon \le \frac{\delta ^3}{ 50} \,, \quad \gamma = \frac{\delta }{8} \,, \quad \sigma _q = a_q^{1 + \gamma } \,, \quad a_{q+1} =a_q^{1+ \delta }\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a0ϵδ/8≤120,ϵ≤δ350,γ=δ8,σq=aq1+γ,aq+1=aq1+δ,and that the length of the time intervals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}_{q,j}$$\end{document}Iq,j is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_q^{\gamma - \gamma \delta }$$\end{document}aqγ-γδ (for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \in m{{\mathbb {N}}}$$\end{document}q∈mN and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=0$$\end{document}j=0), 0 (for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \not \in m{{\mathbb {N}}}$$\end{document}q∉mN and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=0$$\end{document}j=0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_q^\gamma $$\end{document}aqγ otherwise.

We consider the initial datum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{{{\,\textrm{in}\,}}}$$\end{document}ϑin defined in (4.17) and the velocity field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in C^\alpha ((0,2) \times {{\mathbb {T}}}^2)$$\end{document}u∈Cα((0,2)×T2) constructed in Section 4.2 for times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le t \le 1$$\end{document}0≤t≤1 and extended for times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le t \le 2$$\end{document}1≤t≤2 by formula (4.15) (in particular we make use of the swap velocity field). For the regularity of the velocity field see Remark 4.2.

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _q$$\end{document}σq we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{\sigma _q}$$\end{document}ϑσq be the unique solution of the advection equation (1.1) with velocity field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \star \varphi _{\sigma _q}$$\end{document}u⋆φσq and initial datum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{{{\,\textrm{in}\,}}}$$\end{document}ϑin as in (4.17), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C^\infty _c ((-1,1) \times B(0,1))$$\end{document}φ∈Cc∞((-1,1)×B(0,1)) is the convolution kernel in space-time fixed in the statement of the theorem. We observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \vartheta _{\sigma _q} \Vert _{L^\infty ((0,2) \times {{\mathbb {T}}}^2)} \le \Vert \vartheta _0 \Vert _{L^\infty ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2)} \le 1$$\end{document}‖ϑσq‖L∞((0,2)×T2)≤‖ϑ0‖L∞(T2)≤1 for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \in {{\mathbb {N}}}$$\end{document}q∈N.

In the following lemma we prove an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\infty $$\end{document}L∞ bound on the mollified velocity field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \star \varphi _\sigma $$\end{document}u⋆φσ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in [a_{q+1},a_q]$$\end{document}σ∈[aq+1,aq] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (1-T_q,1+T_q)$$\end{document}t∈(1-Tq,1+Tq). The choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _q$$\end{document}σq as in (4.8) guarantees the uniform smallness of the velocity field in such time interval.

Lemma 5.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u: (0,2) \times {{\mathbb {T}}}^2 \rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}^2$$\end{document}u:(0,2)×T2→R2 be the velocity field defined above and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C^\infty _c ((-1,1) \times B(0,1))$$\end{document}φ∈Cc∞((-1,1)×B(0,1)) be a convolution kernel in space-time. Then, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \in {{\mathbb {N}}}$$\end{document}q∈N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{q+1} \le \sigma \le a_q$$\end{document}aq+1≤σ≤aq we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \star \varphi _\sigma \Vert _{L^\infty ((1-T_q, 1+ T_q ) \times {{\mathbb {T}}}^2)} \le \frac{ \overline{C} \Vert \varphi \Vert _{C^1} a_{q+1} a_q^{1- \gamma }}{\sigma }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u⋆φσ‖L∞((1-Tq,1+Tq)×T2)≤C¯‖φ‖C1aq+1aq1-γσ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{C}>0$$\end{document}C¯>0 is a universal constant.

Proof

Let us fix q and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ as in the statement. We first estimate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u \star \varphi _\sigma ) ( t, x)$$\end{document}(u⋆φσ)(t,x) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in {{\mathbb {T}}}^2$$\end{document}x∈T2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in \mathcal {I}_{q,2}$$\end{document}t∈Iq,2. Recalling (4.13) we define the set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Z = \{(y_1, y_2) \in B (x, 2 \sigma ) : w_{q+1, 2}(y_2 ) = - w_{q+1,2}( y_2 + a_{q+1}) \} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Z={(y1,y2)∈B(x,2σ):wq+1,2(y2)=-wq+1,2(y2+aq+1)}.It holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}^2 (Z^c \cap B(x, 2 \sigma )) \le 64 a_{q+1} \sigma $$\end{document}L2(Zc∩B(x,2σ))≤64aq+1σ since the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z^c \cap B(x, 2 \sigma )$$\end{document}Zc∩B(x,2σ) is made of at most 16 horizontal stripes of length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4\sigma $$\end{document}4σ and height \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{q+1}$$\end{document}aq+1 thanks to the construction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{q+1,2}$$\end{document}wq+1,2. Using (4.20b) we estimate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\left| (u \star \varphi _\sigma ) (t, x) \right| = \frac{1}{2} \left| \int _{-1}^1 \int _{{{\mathbb {T}}}^2} \left[ u(s, y)\varphi _\sigma (t -s, x - y ) + u(s, y + a_{q+1} \textbf{e}_2 )\varphi _\sigma (t -s, x - y + a_{q+1} \textbf{e}_2 ) \right] dy ds \right| \\&\le \frac{1}{2} \left| \int _{-1}^1 \int _{Z} \left[ u(s, y)\varphi _\sigma (t -s, x - y ) + u(s, y + a_{q+1} \textbf{e}_2)\varphi _\sigma (t -s, x - y + a_{q+1} \textbf{e}_2 ) \right] dy ds \right| \\&\quad + \frac{1}{2} \left| \int _{-1}^1 \int _{Z^c \cap B(x, 2 \sigma )} \big [ u(s, y)\varphi _\sigma (t -s, x - y ) + u(s, y + a_{q+1} \textbf{e}_2)\varphi _\sigma (t -s, x - y + a_{q+1} \textbf{e}_2) \big ] dy ds \right| \\&\le \frac{ \Vert u \Vert _{L^\infty ({\mathcal {I}}_{q,2} \times {{\mathbb {T}}}^2)}}{2} \int _{-1}^1 \int _{Z} | \varphi _\sigma (t -s, x - y ) - \varphi _\sigma (t -s, x - y + a_{q+1} \textbf{e}_2 ) | dy ds\\&\quad + 2 \mathcal {L}^2 (Z^c \cap B(x, 2 \sigma )) \Vert u \Vert _{L^\infty ({\mathcal {I}}_{q,2} \times {{\mathbb {T}}}^2)} \int _{-1}^1 \Vert \varphi _\sigma (s,\cdot ) \Vert _{L^\infty ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2)} ds\\&\le \frac{a_q^{1- \gamma } 4 \pi \Vert \nabla \varphi \Vert _{L^\infty } a_{q+1}}{ \sigma } + \frac{256 a_{q+1} \sigma a_q^{1- \gamma } \Vert \varphi \Vert _{L^\infty }}{\sigma ^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(u⋆φσ)(t,x)=12∫-11∫T2u(s,y)φσ(t-s,x-y)+u(s,y+aq+1e2)φσ(t-s,x-y+aq+1e2)dyds≤12∫-11∫Zu(s,y)φσ(t-s,x-y)+u(s,y+aq+1e2)φσ(t-s,x-y+aq+1e2)dyds+12∫-11∫Zc∩B(x,2σ)[u(s,y)φσ(t-s,x-y)+u(s,y+aq+1e2)φσ(t-s,x-y+aq+1e2)]dyds≤‖u‖L∞(Iq,2×T2)2∫-11∫Z|φσ(t-s,x-y)-φσ(t-s,x-y+aq+1e2)|dyds+2L2(Zc∩B(x,2σ))‖u‖L∞(Iq,2×T2)∫-11‖φσ(s,·)‖L∞(T2)ds≤aq1-γ4π‖∇φ‖L∞aq+1σ+256aq+1σaq1-γ‖φ‖L∞σ2.The same estimate holds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in \mathcal {J}_{q,2}$$\end{document}t∈Jq,2. Finally, we observe that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in I= \mathcal {I}_{q,3} \cup \mathcal {J}_{q,3} \cup [1-T_{q+1}, 1+ T_{q+1}] $$\end{document}t∈I=Iq,3∪Jq,3∪[1-Tq+1,1+Tq+1] the estimate follows from the bound\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \Vert _{L^\infty (I \times {{\mathbb {T}}}^2)} \le 2 a_{q+1} a_q^{- \gamma } \le \frac{256 \Vert \varphi \Vert _{C^1} a_{q+1} a_q^{1- \gamma }}{\sigma } \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u‖L∞(I×T2)≤2aq+1aq-γ≤256‖φ‖C1aq+1aq1-γσ.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Proof of Theorem C

In the first three steps of the proof we fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \in {{\mathbb {N}}}$$\end{document}q∈N sufficiently large and we describe (up to small errors that we explicitly quantify) the flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{X}}^{\sigma _q}$$\end{document}Xσq on the time intervals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,1-T_q]$$\end{document}[0,1-Tq], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[1-T_q, 1+T_q]$$\end{document}[1-Tq,1+Tq], and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[1+ T_q, 2]$$\end{document}[1+Tq,2], respectively. In the last step we prove that the two subsequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{\sigma _{2q}}$$\end{document}ϑσ2q and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{\sigma _{2q+1}}$$\end{document}ϑσ2q+1 cannot converge (with respect to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {weak}^*$$\end{document}weak∗ topology) to the same limit as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}q→∞.

We split the proof in four steps. Convergence to a Solution which Dissipates the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 Norm in Theorem A and Theorem B

We consider the initial datum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{{{\,\textrm{in}\,}}}$$\end{document}ϑin defined in (4.17) and the velocity field u constructed in Section 4.2 for times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le t \le 1$$\end{document}0≤t≤1 and extended for times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le t \le 2$$\end{document}1≤t≤2 by formula (4.14). For the regularity of the velocity field in the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =0$$\end{document}β=0 see Remark 4.2, whereas in the general setting of Theorem A see Section 8.

We let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{\tilde{\kappa }_q}$$\end{document}ϑκ~q be the unique bounded solution of the advection-diffusion equation (1.2) with velocity field u, initial datum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{{{\,\textrm{in}\,}}}$$\end{document}ϑin, and diffusivity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\kappa }_q$$\end{document}κ~q.

We split the proof in two steps. Proof of the Regularity in Theorem A

In this final section we prove the regularity of the velocity field u and the uniform-in-diffusivity regularity estimate (1.7) for the solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{\kappa }$$\end{document}ϑκ of the advection-diffusion equation (1.2), therefore concluding the proof of Theorem A.

We begin with an elementary, but crucial, observation. Consider an horizontal or vertical shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{u} \in C^{\infty }([0,1] \times {{\mathbb {T}}}^2)$$\end{document}u¯∈C∞([0,1]×T2) and consider the unique stochastic flow solving (3.4). Then we claim that8.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{\omega \in \Omega } \Vert {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa }_{0,t }( \cdot , \omega ) \Vert _{W^{1, \infty }} \le 3 + \int _0^t \Vert \nabla \overline{u} (s,\cdot ) \Vert _{ L^\infty } ds \quad \text{ for } \text{ any } t \in [0,1]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supω∈Ω‖X0,tκ(·,ω)‖W1,∞≤3+∫0t‖∇u¯(s,·)‖L∞dsforanyt∈[0,1].Indeed, let us consider without loss of generality the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{u}(t, x,y) = (\overline{u}(t, y), 0)$$\end{document}u¯(t,x,y)=(u¯(t,y),0), with a slight abuse of notation. The backward stochastic flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{X}}^{\kappa }_{0,t} = ({\varvec{X}}^{\kappa ,1}_{0,t}, {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa , 2}_{0,t})$$\end{document}X0,tκ=(X0,tκ,1,X0,tκ,2) can be explicitly computed as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa ,1}_{0,t} (x,y, \omega ) = x + \displaystyle \int _t^0 \overline{u} (s, {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa ,2}_{0,s} (x, y, \omega ) ) ds - \sqrt{2 \kappa } {\varvec{W}}\hspace{-2.22214pt}_t^{\ 1} (\omega ) \,\\ {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa ,2}_{0,t} (x,y, \omega ) = y - \sqrt{2 \kappa } {\varvec{W}}\hspace{-2.22214pt}_t^{\ 2} (\omega ) \,. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X0,tκ,1(x,y,ω)=x+∫t0u¯(s,X0,sκ,2(x,y,ω))ds-2κWt1(ω)X0,tκ,2(x,y,ω)=y-2κWt2(ω).Hence, the derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _y {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa }_{0,t} (x,y, \omega )$$\end{document}∂yX0,tκ(x,y,ω) can be estimated as follows\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\left| {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa ,1 }_{0,t} (x, y + h, \omega ) - {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa ,1}_{0,t} (x, y , \omega ) \right| \\&\quad \le \left| \int _0^t \overline{u} (s, y +h - \sqrt{2 \kappa } {\varvec{W}}\hspace{-2.22214pt}_s^{\ 2}(\omega ) ) - \overline{u} (s, y - \sqrt{2 \kappa } {\varvec{W}}\hspace{-2.22214pt}_s^{\ 2}(\omega ) ) ds \right| \\&\le |h| \int _0^t \Vert \nabla \overline{u} (s,\cdot ) \Vert _{ L^\infty } ds, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X0,tκ,1(x,y+h,ω)-X0,tκ,1(x,y,ω)≤∫0tu¯(s,y+h-2κWs2(ω))-u¯(s,y-2κWs2(ω))ds≤|h|∫0t‖∇u¯(s,·)‖L∞ds,while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{X}}^{\kappa , 2}_{0,t }(x, y + h, \omega ) - {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa ,2 }_{0,t} (x,y, \omega ) \equiv h$$\end{document}X0,tκ,2(x,y+h,ω)-X0,tκ,2(x,y,ω)≡h. Noticing that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{X}}^{\kappa }_{0,t }(x+ h, y , \omega ) - {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa }_{0,t} (x,y, \omega ) \equiv (h,0)$$\end{document}X0,tκ(x+h,y,ω)-X0,tκ(x,y,ω)≡(h,0) we conclude that (8.1) holds.

Proof of the regularity in Theorem A

We first prove that we \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in L^p ((0,1); C^\alpha ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2))$$\end{document}u∈Lp((0,1);Cα(T2)). Indeed, by (4.20)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \Vert _{L^p C^\alpha }^p&= \sum _{q=1}^\infty \int _{{\mathcal {I}}_q} \Vert u (s,\cdot )\Vert _{C^\alpha ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2)}^{p} ds \le \sum _{q=1}^\infty \int _{{\mathcal {I}}_q} \Vert u (s,\cdot )\Vert _{L^\infty ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2)}^{p (1- \alpha )} \Vert u (s,\cdot )\Vert _{W^{1,\infty }({{\mathbb {T}}}^2)}^{p\alpha } ds\\&\lesssim \sum _{q=0}^\infty \lambda _q^{- \gamma } \lambda _q^{p(1- \alpha )(\gamma -1) } \lambda _q^{p \alpha (\gamma -1) } \lambda _{q+1}^{p \alpha (1 + \epsilon \delta )} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u‖LpCαp=∑q=1∞∫Iq‖u(s,·)‖Cα(T2)pds≤∑q=1∞∫Iq‖u(s,·)‖L∞(T2)p(1-α)‖u(s,·)‖W1,∞(T2)pαds≲∑q=0∞λq-γλqp(1-α)(γ-1)λqpα(γ-1)λq+1pα(1+ϵδ)and the sum is finite if and only if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\gamma }{p} + 1 - \gamma - \alpha (1 + \epsilon \delta ) (1 + \delta ) >0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}γp+1-γ-α(1+ϵδ)(1+δ)>0which indeed holds thanks to the choice (4.4), the condition (4.1a) and the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${p}^\circ \ge 2$$\end{document}p∘≥2.

We now turn to the proof of the uniform-in-diffusivity bound in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{p^\circ }((0,1); C^\beta ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2))$$\end{document}Lp∘((0,1);Cβ(T2)) for the solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _{\kappa }$$\end{document}ϑκ of (1.2). It is sufficient to prove that the (backward) stochastic flow is such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa }_{0, \cdot } ( \cdot , \omega ) \Vert _{L^{p^\circ } C^\beta }$$\end{document}‖X0,·κ(·,ω)‖Lp∘Cβ is uniformly bounded independently on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ. If this is the case, from the Feynman-Kac formula (3.10) we have8.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} | \vartheta _{\kappa } (t,x ) - \vartheta _{\kappa } (t,y)|&\le \int _{\Omega } | \vartheta _{{{\,\textrm{in}\,}}} ({\varvec{X}}^{\kappa }_{0,t} (x, \omega )) - \vartheta _{{{\,\textrm{in}\,}}} ({\varvec{X}}^{\kappa }_{0,t} (y, \omega )) | d\mathbb {P}(\omega ) \nonumber \\&\le \Vert \nabla \vartheta _{{{\,\textrm{in}\,}}} \Vert _{L^\infty } \int _{\Omega } | {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa }_{0,t} (x, \omega ) - {\varvec{X}}^{\kappa }_{0,t} (y, \omega ) | d\mathbb {P}(\omega ) \nonumber \\&\le \Vert \nabla \vartheta _{{{\,\textrm{in}\,}}} \Vert _{L^\infty } \sup _{\omega \in \Omega } \Vert {\varvec{X}}^\kappa _{0,t} (\cdot , \omega ) \Vert _{C^\beta } |x- y|^\beta \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|ϑκ(t,x)-ϑκ(t,y)|≤∫Ω|ϑin(X0,tκ(x,ω))-ϑin(X0,tκ(y,ω))|dP(ω)≤‖∇ϑin‖L∞∫Ω|X0,tκ(x,ω)-X0,tκ(y,ω)|dP(ω)≤‖∇ϑin‖L∞supω∈Ω‖X0,tκ(·,ω)‖Cβ|x-y|βfrom which we conclude that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \vartheta _\kappa (t, \cdot ) \Vert _{C^\beta ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2)} \lesssim \sup _{\omega \in \Omega } \Vert {\varvec{X}}^\kappa _{0,t} (\cdot , \omega ) \Vert _{C^\beta ({{\mathbb {T}}}^2)}$$\end{document}‖ϑκ(t,·)‖Cβ(T2)≲supω∈Ω‖X0,tκ(·,ω)‖Cβ(T2) for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in [0,1]$$\end{document}t∈[0,1].

Let us show the regularity of the backward stochastic flow.
